Not only does it make the UK's economy dangerously dependent on a handful of lucrative Londonbased service industries like finance, law, and consulting, it's also politically toxic because the UK's other regions understandably feel left behind, which is why Boris Johnson's talk of leveling up was so electorally successful back in 2019. And as is the case in most developed economies, consumption accounts for a majority of all economic activity in the UK. Both the US and the Euro area, for instance, saw consumption rates returned to trend after the pandemic. ## 6. The UK's Energy Crisis Explained

If you ask your average Brit what's gone wrong in the UK, they'll probably mention high taxes, the near perma crisis in the NHS, maybe the cost of living or student loans. They're unlikely, however, to mention energy, despite the fact that the UK currently suffers from some of the highest energy prices in the world, and that this has a penicious effect on the economy more broadly. So, in this video, we're going to look at the UK's oftlooked but very serious energy crisis, how we got here, and why, depressing as this might be, there is a silver lining. We're in the process of clearing out our store, so we're running a fire sale right now. Using code firetell, you can get 40% off all of our posters, notebooks, calendars, magnets, keyrings, Brexit, the coloring book, the lot. The offer ends very soon, so click the link in the description for more. So, let's get straight into it. This is a bit of a generalization, but broadly speaking, energy consumption can be split into two forms. direct fuel consumption, which includes burning fuels for stuff like transport and heating, and electricity consumption, which accounts for an ever growing share of total energy consumption. The UK doesn't do too badly on the first front. Petrol prices in the UK, for instance, are broadly on a par with the rest of Europe, as are industrial gas prices. It's on the second front, however, where things look rubbish. Electricity in the UK is, to be frank, stupidly expensive. According to UK government data, electricity costs 27 p per kilowatt hour for industrial users, the highest in the developed world by some distance and 30 p per kilowatt hour for domestic users, second only to Germany. It didn't used to be this way. As recently as the early 2010s, UK electricity prices weren't much higher than elsewhere. It's hard to overstate quite how terrible this is for the UK economy. High electricity costs have contributed to the UK's acute de-industrialization, pushed up inflation, and crippled total energy consumption, which generally correlates with lower overall GDP. Ironically, high electricity prices also make it harder to decarbonize because they discourage electrification. So, what went wrong? Well, commentators often blame the UK's so-called marginal pricing system. For context, every 30 minutes there's an auction where electricity generators submit bids, essentially describing at what price they could generate electricity. The system operator then ranks each generator by price and then goes up that ranking saying, "Yes, we'll need your electricity and your electricity and your electricity until they've got enough to meet demand." Importantly, however, in a marginal pricing system, the wholesale price of electricity is set by the most expensive source required. There are at least two problems with this system. First, while it made sense when most of our electricity was produced by burning fossil fuels and there wasn't much difference in cost across generators, it makes less sense now that our electricity comes from lots of different sources offering very different prices. If for instance, solar generators say they can generate at 5 p per kowatt hour and gas generators say 20 p. Unless solar and wind can power up the entire UK, the system operator ends up paying 20 p per kilowatt hour, even though the average generation cost is way lower. The second problem is that because solar, wind, and other sources are rarely plentiful enough to meet total demand, the UK usually needs gas generators to step in, who end up setting the wholesale price. In fact, according to a paper published by UCL in 2023, between 2015 and 2021, gas ended up setting the wholesale price 98% of the time in the UK, more than any other European country. This leaves the UK electricity market uniquely vulnerable to changes in international gas prices, as we saw after Putin's invasion of Ukraine, and explains why wholesale electricity prices have broadly tracked international gas prices in recent years. So, you get the idea. People often blame the UK's sky-high electricity prices on our marginal pricing system and the fact that in this system, gas generators usually end up setting the price. But this isn't a sufficient explanation for at least two reasons. For starters, this alone doesn't explain why prices are so high. After all, lots of other countries also rely on gas. According to that paper we just mentioned, Italy, the Netherlands, Belgium, and Greece all rely on gas. at least 80% of the time. Furthermore, that paper probably understates other count's reliance on gas because when countries like Ireland import electricity to meet demand, they're usually implicitly relying on gas. And when other countries like Spain use hydropower, this is often essentially price matched to gas. And yet all these countries enjoy significantly lower electricity prices than the UK. So why is this? Well, quite a lot of this has to do with the fact that the marginal pricing system is even more broken than we just implied. This is because once the system operator has accepted the bids and decided who gets paid, they then look at a map to see if this is actually going to work. Sometimes it doesn't. Quite often, for instance, all the wind farms in Scotland are generating lots of cheap electricity, but our grid can't carry all that electricity down to where it's needed in more populated southern areas. This means that quite often Scottish wind farms have their bids accepted, but the operator then says, "Sorry, the grid can't actually carry your electricity down to where it's needed. These wind farms still get paid because they had their bids accepted, but they don't actually generate any electricity. The operator then has to go find another generator nearer the demand centers to fill the gap, which will be by definition even more expensive." Again, a few decades ago, this wasn't really a problem because our coal and gas powered stations were spread relatively evenly throughout the UK. But it's becoming more of a problem with time. Both because renewables are generally concentrated in certain geographically convenient areas and because our grid is outdated with these so-called balancing costs expected to quadruple between 2022 and 2026. You get the idea. It's not just our reliance on gas. It's also our uniquely dysfunctional energy market. But this all points to an even deeper question. Why are we so reliant on gas in the first place? Well, this is a difficult question and would probably require a whole other video. But the TLDDR is that there aren't any easy alternatives. Our nuclear industry, for instance, is massively overregulated, as evidenced by the high and rising cost of plant construction. Solar doesn't work brilliantly in the UK because while solar panels are becoming incredibly cheap, it's just not that sunny here. The UK is pretty windy, but the cost of new wind farms is actually rising, at least according to the most recent government auctions. So, that's at least a partial explanation of why energy prices in the UK are so high. As a final thing, there is a massive silver lining here. Namely, that it is frankly astonishing that we are as rich as we are while using as little energy as we do. Usually, energy consumption tracks GDP per capita. The UK is one of very few countries to break this trend. Our energy consumption has plummeted and yet our GDP per capita has held steady. Today, our GDP per units of energy is better than that of every other large economy. implying that with even slightly more plentiful energy, the UK could be one of the richest countries in the world. Established in 1990, the student loan system was designed to provide students with lowinterest loans in order to make going to university more accessible. Since then though, and after numerous alterations, we're now at a point where the entire system seems broken. Last year, the total interest added to England's student loans was 15 billion pounds, around three times the amount that students actually paid off. And student debt write offs increased by 415% this year alone, with the number expected to increase by about 100 times by the 2040s. But how did we get here? How have we got to a situation where the system is this broken? We're in the process of clearing out our store, so we're running a fire sale right now. Using code fire sale, you can get 40% off all of our posters, notebooks, calendars, magnets, keyrings, Brexit the coloring book, the lot. That offer ends very soon, so click the link in the description for more. Before we get into how broken the system is, we need to understand how it works. Student loans in the UK are managed by the creatively named Student Loan Company, which provides universities with money upfront on behalf of students and administers their repayment plans. Depending on when and where a student went to university, they will fit into one of five student loan plans. Each one of these plans has a repayment threshold, which is the income at which they will start repaying their loan. In theory, these repayment thresholds should be moved up regularly to prevent something known as fiscal drag, where inflation or wage growth pushes more people into paying. However, in last year's budget, Rachel Reeves confirmed that the repayment threshold for those on plan two would be frozen for 3 years. Now, everyone on plans 1 2 4 and five will repay 9% of their income above their respective thresholds. and those on plan three post-graduate loans will pay 6%. Each plan also has a write-off period, the time period after which any amount still left on the loan is written off. This is usually around 30 years, but for some plans it's slightly shorter and for some it's slightly longer. So far so easy, right? The thing that makes student loans complicated though is the interest rates. Certain plans have much more favorable interest rates. The interest rate on plan 1 and four, for instance, is based on either the retail price index RPI or the Bank of England base rate plus 1%. Whichever is lower. This can lead to some pretty extortionate interest rates when inflation is high because high inflation usually means high interest rates as well as the Bank of England tries to bring prices back down. This is all exacerbated by the fact that the retail price index has a habit of overestimating the change in prices largely due to the formula used to calculate it. According to ONS, this so-called formula effect adds on roughly 0.7 percentage points to retail price index when compared to the consumer price index, which is what we usually use to measure inflation, which is why the ONS has previously advised against using RPI more generally. Despite this, it is still used for calculating interest on student loans. For those on plans one and four, this is bad. But for those on plan two student loans, this is devastating. Those on plan two, English students who went to university between 2012 and 2023 can, depending on their income, pay an interest rate as high as RPI plus 3%. In 2022, RPI skyrocketed to more than 9%. Meaning that the higher earners on plan 2 student loans would have seen the interest on their loan rise to a whopping 12%. Now, the government did implement a cap of 7.1%, but this slowly rose, reaching a high of 7.9% in 2024, without which the interest rate on student loans could have been as high as 16%. As interest rates have fallen since, the government has scrapped the cap. But the maximum interest rate for those on plan 2 still stands at 6.2%. It's worth remembering here though that that interest is compounding. Those high interest rates in 2022 2023 are still affecting the overall size of student loan debts for many students. Clearly, the government recognized that the system was broken as in 2023, they introduced plan 5 student loans for English students who went to university from 2023 onward, scrapping the additional plus 3% sliding scale from plan 2. This though does little to help the situation. Plan 5 comes with its own problems, including an extended write-off period and a lower repayment threshold. And those with plan two student loans are still stuck with the same lousy interest rate system as before. In many ways, the ridiculous levels of interest create something of a regressive taxation system. Those who are able to pay off their student loans are able to avoid the crazy interest rate and will pay less tax than someone who takes longer to pay it off. Up until 2017 2018, graduates were collectively paying off more each year than the amount of interest added, albeit not by much. Since then, each year graduates have had more interest added than they paid off. With this spiking in 2022 2023, and in the last 2 years, the amount of interest added has been more than triple the amount that graduates actually repaid. For many students and graduates, then it's seeming less and less likely that they will ever pay off their student loans. The government itself predicts that only 32% of those in the 2022 2023 cohort, i.e. those still on plan 2, will fully pay off their loans. Though due to the lower interest rate of those on the new plan 5 loans and the longer write-off period, the government predicts that 56% of those in the 2024 2025 cohort will fully pay back their loans. This means that even under the new system, nearly half of students will be stuck paying 9% of their income above a certain threshold into their 60s. Not only is this terrible for the individual, but it's also bad for public finances. Already, student loan write offs are starting to skyrocket to around30 million, a4% increase in just a year. However, this is just the start. When the first 2011 2012 cohort reaches their student loan write-off date in the early 2040s, 30 years after their loan was issued, the write-offs are expected to increase to30 billion pounds a year, more than 100 times what it is today. Now, fortunately, this has already been factored in. In 2018, the ONS changed the rules so that the proportion of student loans expected to be written off accounted as a government expenditure. However, this still means that each year between now and 203031, student loan rights will deduct an average of 10 billion pounds from the public finances. As such, in recent months, there's been growing calls within Labour for Bernham to return to Westminster and replace Stara as leader given his dire approval ratings. However, the first step was for Burnham to win a seat and become a Labour MP. For months, journalists were watching the constituency of Gorton and Denton, a Manchester constituency that was held by Labour MP Andrew Gwyn, who was suspended in February last year for making disparaging remarks about constituents. The last mayoral election cost 4.7 million pounds and that it would require the party to split their resources across two separate bi-elections. Second, at a time when reformer outpolling labor by 10 points nationally, Labour were concerned that reform could win the Manchester Mayoral bi-election and as such it would be better to keep Burnham in place. Click the link in the description for the full collection and get 20% off for a limited time using code YouTube 20. Now, let's start by explaining how driving tests in the UK actually work. Essentially, in order to legally drive a car on UK road, citizens need to pass two tests. the theory test and the practical test. The theory test is a computer-based test that takes place at a specifically designed test center. And the practical test is a test of someone's actual driving abilities conducted by a driving examiner. Prior to 2020, the number of both kinds of tests remain relatively stable yearbyear. Around 2 million car theory tests conducted each year and around 1.6 million practical tests. But during the first and second lockdowns of the CO9 pandemic, all tests, both theory and practical, were cancelled due to public health concerns, creating a backlog of 1.1 million untaken tests, which won't be fully cleared until November 2027. Fortunately, the theory tests bounce back pretty quickly after the pandemic. In fact, in the years since, the number of theory tests conducted has actually increased by around 200,000 a quarter, which is useful considering that during the pandemic itself, there was a 52.2% 2% decrease in theory tests versus the previous year. What this means is that according to the most recent estimates by the driver and vehicle standards agency, the executive agency in charge of managing driving tests, between 2024 and 2025, people waited an average of only 3.3 weeks for a theory test, comfortably below the 4-we target. However, this was not the same for the practical driving tests. The pandemic caused significantly more disruption to these kinds of tests with a 72.7% decrease in the number of practical tests in the pandemic year versus the prior year. This is mainly due to the fact that when tests were allowed again between lockdowns 1 and two, only around 200,000 were conducted per quarter, half the normal rate. And while in the years since the pandemic, the number of tests administered has returned to normal, they haven't increase to make up for this shortfall. Considering that the backlog in 202021 stood at 1.1 million, this just hasn't been enough to clear it. Even now, 5 years on from the pandemic, the backlog is estimated to be 360,000. As a result of this, the DVSA has shown that on average, people are waiting 21.9 weeks for a practical driving test, an increase from the 16.1 week average in 2023 24 and significantly above the 7we target. So, why has the number of theory tests been able to increase to meet demand while practical tests just haven't? Well, it's likely due to the nature of the tests themselves. Practical tests require a physical driving examiner in the car, whereas only a small number of physical employees are required to oversee a big group of people sitting the theory test. It is therefore significantly easier to scale up to meet the new demand for more theory tests than it is for the practical ones. But the issue with practical driving test waiting list specifically is that it encourages actions both by driving instructors and learners that make the wait list even longer. Driving instructors aware of the significant backlog are booking tests as soon as they take on new students, holding on to said bookings and even transferring them to students who are ready to take their practical tests. Under the current rules, this is completely above board as individuals are able to change the details of their driving test slots up to six times without charge as long as the changes are made 3 days before the time of the actual booking. This would be bad if it was just driving instructors doing it, but there are plenty of reports of people buying driving tests for £65 and then selling them onto other people for up to £500. Just to give a sense of the scale at which this is happening, back in 2019, only 39,000 tests were swapped. But by 2024, this had risen to 327,000, a nearly 10fold increase. Obviously, the only reason that any of this is happening is because there's a shortage of tests. But what this does is actually reduce the availability of tests even further. That's why the government has confirmed that in spring it'll be changing the rules that only the learner is able to book the tests. The number of changes allowed will be reduced to two, and that changes will have to be made at the latest, 10 working days before the test. They've also proposed a new rule which would see learners made to wait 6 months after taking their theory test before they can take their practical test. The problem with this approach is that these are demandside fixes. It should free up some availability, but it won't fix the overall cause of the problem, but there just aren't enough driving examiners. While the government had previously committed to hiring an additional 450 driving examiners, it so far managed to net just 83 despite 19 different recruitment campaigns since 2021. Government figures show that around 4,600 people currently working at the DVSA, although we don't know how many of them are driving examiners. So, an increase of 450 would actually have been fairly significant, increasing the overall headcount of the DVSA by about 10%. Unfortunately though, as I just mentioned, the government didn't manage it. So, why not? Well, a lot of it comes down to the fact that driving examiners just aren't that well paid, and it's hard to entice people into the job. A Freedom of Information request from 2015 revealed that the salary for driving examiners was between £24,484 and £25,78, a couple of,000 less than the average UK salary in 2015, which was £27,600. In 2025, however, the starting salary for driving examiners was around £28,000, significantly below the £39,100 average salary last year. When you consider that driving instructors are self-employed and typically earn up to £40,000 a year, it's not surprising that there's a shortage of driving examiners. So, if the government wants to reduce the driving test crisis, it needs to not only address the demand side issues, but the supply side issues, too. namely by paying driving examiners more. Users have reported a notable uptick in hate speech and disinformation as a result. However, when contacted about the situation, X replied to a number of requests from news outlets with the automated reply, "Legacy media lies," while Elon Musk replied to a number of AI edits of famous people, including himself in bikinis, with cry laugh emojis. Clearly, the initial response by the company and the CEO of X weren't indicative of a company taking the situation seriously. In the following days, numerous governments and organizations publicly criticized X for their lack of action. The Internet Watch Foundation or IWF, for instance, claimed that criminal imagery, likely classified as category C under UK law, was being created by Grock and claimed that the chatbot risks bringing sexual AI imagery of children into the mainstream. Essentially, the online safety act of 2023 put new duties on social media companies to ensure that illegal content isn't seen and created by the platform and that any illegal content is taken down swiftly. Specifically, it states that the platforms are obligated to take or use proportionate measures to prevent individuals from encountering priority illegal content, which includes intimate image abuse and child sexual abuse material. mitigate and manage the risk of the service being used for the commission or facilitation of a priority offense and implement systems and processes designed to minimize the length of time for which any priority illegal content is present. On the former of these powers, given that X was expected to have taken in around $2 billion in the first nine months of 2025, if Ofcom opted to go down this route, they'd be able to impose a fine of at least $200 million or about $150 million. The latter of these powers, the business disruption measures are much more significant. Essentially, when all other options fail, they can be used to limit the harm that a service can provide. Offcom can apply to the courts to introduce either a service restriction order which targets infrastructure that supports a non-compliant service or an access restriction order which can be used to essentially ban the service by telling internet service providers to block it. Actually going down the access restriction order route which would block X in the UK would be virtually unprecedented. It would remove access for 20 million UK users and would almost certainly cause another rift with the Trump administration. Sarah Rogers, the US State Department's under secretary for public diplomacy, for instance, has already stated that the UK is contemplating a Russia style Xban to protect them from bikini images. Now, we're not saying that offcom wouldn't ban X, but rather we're saying that it's likely we'll see Offcom attempt a more hands-off approach as the first step before slowly ratcheting up to an overall ban. In the meantime, the government will likely use other powers to try and discourage and criminalize individuals for using Grock to create such images. ## 12. Could the Lords Block the Chagos Deal?
**Channel:** TLDR News | **Views:** 125K | **Date:** 1 month ago | **Duration:** 8:20 | **ID:** oyjH_I66blA
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=oyjH_I66blA

### Transcript:
Back in May 2025, the UK government signed a deal to hand over the Chaos Islands to Maitius while leasing Diego Garcia, the largest of the islands and one that houses a joint UK US military base for 99 years for the cost of on average 101 million a year. While the debate about the pros and cons of such a deal have raged on since, there seems to have been very little progress on it. That is until this week. On Monday, the Diego Garcia Military Base and British Indian Ocean Territory Bill, the bill that ratifies the deal, went through the House of Lords and was amended four times. So, in this video, we're going to go through a quick refresher on what the Chaos Islands deal is, explain a little about why it's controversial, why the Lords made changes to the deal, and whether ultimately the Lords could block or seriously alter the Chaos deal. >> [music] >> Never miss an episode and always feel in the loop by subscribing and ringing the bell. Now, we should start this video with a brief history of Britain's relationship with the Chaos Islands. The islands were for much of history uninhabited. The first permanent settlements were built in the late 18th century when the French established a copra plantation on the islands using slave labor. These settlements were seeded to the British only a few years later in 1815 and were administered from Maitius. By 1900, around 60% of those living on the island were descendants of the original enslaved population and the other 40% were descendants of indentured laborers from South Asia. Those on the island began to build their own culture. They built towns, churches, schools, and even developed their own Chagosian Creole language. Now, it's important to note here that everyone living on the islands technically worked for the companies that owned and ran the coconut plantations, meaning that only the corporations owned any land on the islands. This was important because in the 1960s, after the UN had called for members to grant independence to their colonies, the UK began secret talks with the US about creating a military base on Diego Garcia, the largest of the Chaos Islands. In order to do this, the UK granted independence to Maitius, but retained the Chaos Islands, incorporating them in 1965 into the British Indian Ocean Territory, or Biot. Maitius was given £3 million to compensate for the separation of Chaos and in 1967, Britain paid £600,000 to the Coconut Plantations, becoming the sole property owner on the islands, and then over 5 years forced all those that lived there out. The UK then leased Diego Garcia to the US, allowing them to construct their military base. Fast forward to 2019, and the UN's International Court of Justice, or ICJ, found that the separation of the islands from Maitius was unlawful and that the UK's continuing rule would be a wrongful act. Although this wasn't legally binding, it was embarrassing and raised fears of further action by the ICJ. It's this fear of a further binding ICJ judgment that pushed the UK into action as it was feared that this could make the base inoperable. A fear that was only made worse as a result of Maitius's close economic ties with China. So in 2022, the Conservatives began negotiations with Maitius about the Chaos Islands. But it was only in 2024, after Labour had entered office, that progress seemed to be made. In October 2024, the UK and Maitius released a joint statement explaining that the Chaos Islands would be handed to Maitius and that the UK would lease Diego Garcia from them for an initial period of 99 years. The deal was only finalized in May of 2025 with it revealed that the UK would pay an average of 101 million to lease Diego Garcia from Maitius with the UK repaying 165 million a year for the first 3 years then 120 million a year plus inflation. The UK also agreed to pay £40 million into a trust to support Chagosians. However, not everyone was happy with the deal. Those on the right claimed that the fears of future binding resolutions from the international community were overblown, while others were frustrated that the deal explicitly prevented the return of the Chagosian people to Diego Garcia. As this deal technically sees British territory, the deal has to be ratified via an act of parliament, meaning that it has to make its way through both the House of Commons and the House of Lords. Through the end of last year, the bill successfully passed through the House of Commons and has since October been making its way through the House of Lords. As the government doesn't have a majority in the Lords, opponents of the deal stood the best chance of changing it in the Lord stages. This is exactly what happened on Monday when the government lost four separate votes on the Chaos deal with each of them significantly altering the wording of the bill. The first of these was a Tory measure that would force the government to publish the total cost of the payments made to Maitius alongside the full methodology used in the calculation. 194 peers voted in favor and 130 voted against. The second amendment that was agreed to was a Liberal Democrat measure that would ensure parliamentary oversight over UK government spending linked to the treaty. The amendment specifically stated that MPs would be able to stop payments to Maitius if they broke the terms of the deal. 131 peers voted in favor and 127 against. The third amendment that was agreed to was another amendment by the Liberal Democrats which would require an amendment among the Chagosian community on whether the transfer deal adequately guaranteed their rights to resettlement. 210 voted in favor and 131 voted against. And lastly, the fourth amendment was an amendment made by Baron Craig of Bradley, a former Royal Air Force commander, that proposed that payments to Maitius would stop if the military base on Diego Garcia could no longer be used, whether that's because of environmental changes, legal restrictions, or an attack by a hostile state. Earlier this week, UK Prime Minister K Star Darmmer hinted at further alignment between the UK and the EU single market, saying it would be preferable to a UK EU customs union. According to the Observer, Downing Street is now exploring the possibility of a Swiss style dynamic realignment deal with the EU, which would involve sectorby sector agreements in the single market rather than pursuing a bespoke customs union as supported by the Liberal Democrats and some senior Labour MPs. So, in this video, we'll explain why Labour wants a Swiss style trading relationship with the EU and how they're going about it. If you want more UK political drama from us, then check out our podcast, Starmageddon, where we track the successes and failures of this new Labor government. Find it over on the TLDDR podcast YouTube channel or in your favorite podcast app. Let's start by explaining both the EU single market and customs union. The EU's customs union includes all 27 EU member states plus Monaco, Andor, San Marino, the British military bases of Acriteri and Dealia in Cyprus and Turkey and consists of two agreements. The first is to remove all tariffs on goods traded between members. And the second is to apply a common external tariff to goods imported from outside the union so that all countries apply the same tariffs to external imports. This facilitates trade by removing the need to prove the origin of goods traded within the customs union but also means that countries are unable to set their own tariff rates reducing their scope to run an independent trade policy. The EU single market, meanwhile, is a common set of laws regulating the movement of goods, services, people, and capital inside the block. It covers all 27 EU member states, plus Norway, Iceland, Likenstein, and Switzerland, and is intended to stimulate trade, fuel growth, and competition, and give businesses greater access to the European market. The EU single market has a GDP of 17.1 trillion euros, representing 15% of global trade, covers€450 million consumers and according to World Bank data is the second largest consumer market in the world after the US. Anyway, after Brexit took effect at the end of 2020, the UK left both the EU single market and customs union. Since then, UK EU trade has been determined by the trade and cooperation agreement or TCA, a free trade agreement that offers the UK tariff-free trade with the EU. However, greater trade friction as a result of Brexit has harmed small businesses, particularly those producing miscellaneous manufacturers like medical equipment, jewelry, sport goods, toys, and office supplies, which saw up to a 30% drop in export to the EU in the 3 years after Brexit. In fact, analysis published in January 2024 by the Center for European Reform, modeling the UK's hypothetical trade if it had stayed in the EU, suggests that UK goods exports to both the EU and rest of the world were around 13% lower than if the UK had remained a member of the EU. Despite the clear impact on trade, Brexit remained a politically sensitive issue. So, in their 2024 manifesto, Labour ruled out rejoining either the EU's customs union or single market, pledging instead to reset relations with the block. Since Labour took office, the UK has signed agreements with the EU on fishing, food standards, youth mobility, carbon pricing, and electricity trading. While Starmer has repeated his manifesto pledge that the UK will not rejoin the EU's single market or customs union. In recent months, however, there have been growing calls for closer UK EU trade relations, driven largely by economic concerns. In early December, the Liberal Democrats narrowly passed a bill requiring the government to start negotiations on a customs union deal with the EU, which while unlikely to become law without government support, was intended to push Labor towards the idea of a UK EU customs union. Indeed, last month several senior Labour figures, including cabinet members David Lammy and West Streeting, expressed support for a UK EU customs union. But this week, Starmmer appeared to turn away from the idea, suggesting the UK should align with the EU single market instead. According to the Observer, Star's team are now exploring a Swiss style dynamic realignment deal, which would give the UK access to the EU single market whilst retaining some independence on trade policy. For context, Switzerland isn't a member of the EU, but has partial access to the EU single market, and its EU trade policy consists of over 120 bilateral agreements in areas like free movement, transport, security, research, and education, with Switzerland adopting certain EU laws and standards in order to align it with the block. So, why is Labour reportedly looking into a Swiss style trade deal with the EU? Well, the main reason is to stimulate economic growth. As we've covered in previous videos, the UK economy is pretty sluggish with November estimates projecting GDP growth of around 1.4% in 2026. Considering Labour's top priority is growing the economy, and they've made seemingly little progress on this, they now appear to be turning their attention towards improving trade with the EU. The government argues that a UK EU customs union might force it to give up the trade deals it's already signed with the US and India by subjecting the UK to the same tariffs as the EU. Whereas greater single market alignment would allow the UK to maintain more freedom over its trade policy. Labour is now planning to introduce a bill to give ministers powers to align with the EU in more areas. Although this could provoke backlash from opposition MPs if it's deemed to go too far. The second reason Labour want a Swiss style trading relationship with the EU is that it would be less politically risky than formally taking the UK back into either the customs union or single market. Yuggov polling from June shows there's very little difference in public sentiment towards rejoining the customs union versus the single market with both options supported by just under half of respondents. But there is notably higher support for a quote closer relationship with the EU without rejoining. A separate Yugov survey actually suggests that if a new referendum were held on whether the UK should rejoin or stay out of the EU, rejoin would win by a clear margin of 8 million votes. However, there are sensible reasons why Labour wouldn't want to reopen the Brexit debate with another vote. Firstly, the potentially toxic nature of another debate on the EU and national sovereignty. And secondly, the fact that one of the key architects of Brexit, former UKIP leader Nigel Farage, is now leading the opinion polls with Reform UK. Ultimately, a deeper trade relationship with the EU, marked by a series of sectorby sector agreements, could help support small businesses in the UK, although any new deals would likely warrant significant scrutiny in Parliament. For the UK government specifically, the high levels of public support for closer relations with the EU and Labour's desperate need for good news suggests they should make this a priority in 2026. ## 14. 9 Years On: How’s Brexit Going?
**Channel:** TLDR News | **Views:** 416K | **Date:** 1 month ago | **Duration:** 9:16 | **ID:** BWplLa3BBX0
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=BWplLa3BBX0

### Transcript:
Every festive season, like most normal people, we at TLDDR get a pang of political nostalgia for the Brexit days of old. We're reminded of Theresa May's first no confidence vote in December 2018, Boris Johnson's landslide victory a year later, and the 1,246page EU UK trade deal published on Boxing Day a year after that, only a couple of days before the transition period expired on New Year's Eve. So, 5 years on from the deal and nearly 10 years on from the referendum, we thought we'd do a little retrospective and take a look at how Brexit's going. Hint, not brilliantly. [music] Longtime TLDDR fans will remember that 5 years ago, we released a book called Brexit, the coloring book. a tongue-in-cheek coins inspired way to relive the Brexit process through drawings and a detailed timeline written by our team. Well, we've reprinted the book, refreshed all of the timeline, and added some extra pages, and it's available right now in our store, and get 20% off for a limited time using code YouTube 20. So, there are sort of two ways of evaluating Brexit, economically and politically. Let's start with the economic impact. As you might remember, in the leadup to the 2016 referendum, a number of prominent commentators and financial institutions warned that leaving the EU or even just voting to leave risked an acute economic crisis and possibly a sudden recession. When this much anticipated crisis failed to immediately materialize, Brexiters declared project fear and interpreted the lack of crisis as proof that Brexit would ultimately deliver the UK to sunlit uplands. Nearly a decade on from the referendum, however, and not only the uplands not looking particularly sunny, but there's now a pretty strong consensus that even if there wasn't an immediate crisis, in the long run, Brexit has been really quite terrible for the UK economy. Two papers published in recent months confirmed this. The first titled The Economic Impact of Brexit 9 years on was published in June by John Springfield at the Center for European Reform and it basically uses what's known as a doppelganger to estimate how the UK economy would have fared without Brexit. For context, in the Brexit related economic literature, a doppelganger is basically a mishmash of other countries that's calibrated to mimic a particular economy, usually the UK. Doppelgangers can help you figure out the impact of some big macroeconomic shock like Brexit because they provide a pretty great independent control. Assuming the countries you used to create the doppelganger in the first place weren't affected by that same shock. The doppelganger allows you to see how the affected economy would have likely performed without that shock. In this paper, Springfield essentially created a doppelganger UK that tracked the UK's economic performance between the financial crisis in 2008 and the Brexit referendum in 2016. Specifically, the doppelganger UK was 31% the US, 15% Germany, 14% New Zealand, 8% Norway, and 5% Australia, with a number of mostly European countries, each accounting for less than 5% of the total, making up the remainder. Springfield then compared the doppelganger's economic performance in the years since the referendum with the UK's actual economic performance to get a sense of how Brexit affected the UK economy. And well, it doesn't make for happy reading. When it comes to GDP growth, the doppelganger grew by about 14% between Brexit in 2023, roughly on par with the US, while the actual UK economy only grew by about 8%, closer to its European neighbors like France and Germany. The implication here is that Brexit has caused something like a 6% hit to the UK's longrun growth rate. The second paper on our list titled simply the economic impact of Brexit and published as a National Bureau of Economic Research working paper in December is even more negative. Using a combination of doppelganger and more traditional forecasting techniques, the authors estimate that by 2025 Brexit had reduced UK GDP by 6 to 8%, even worse than that first paper. This all implies that the OBR's original forecast that Brexit would reduce the UK's long-run productivity by roughly 4% was actually too optimistic despite the fact it was rubbished as yet more project fear in the aftermath of the referendum. That OBR forecast estimated that such a decline would equate to a loss of around £4,000 per household and roughly 40 billion in loss tax revenues. If these papers are to be believed, the real impact could be twice that. Now, you might naturally assume this is mostly a consequence of the impact that Brexit had on the UK's trading relationships. And it is true that Brexit did have a negative effect on UK trade. The UK's total exports, including to both the EU and the rest of the world, have fallen by 15% compared to the doppelganger. The UK's exports to both the EU and the rest of the world fell by similar amounts after Brexit, which is sometimes touted as proof that Brexit didn't actually hurt the UK economy as much as expected, given that you'd expect Brexit to mean a steeper drop in exports to the EU. However, the doppelganger's overperformance suggests a more plausible explanation is that Brexit related disruption to UK EU supply chains both directly reduced the UK's exports to the EU, but also pushed up costs for UK manufacturers more generally, leading to a decline in exports across the board. But while a decline in trade has indeed contributed to the UK's economic underperformance since Brexit, declines in investment as a consequence of Brexit related uncertainty have played at least as big a role. According to that first paper we mentioned, the UK's total private sector investment fell by about 10% compared to the doppelganger. You can sort of see this if you just look at ONS data. investment flatlined after the referendum and has only really returned to its pre206 trend line in the past year or so. This has been truly terrible for the UK economy and at least partly explains why productivity has been so sluggish in the years since. The silver lining here is that both public and private sector investment are finally rising again, meaning the UK economy should see some benefits in the relatively near term. So, you get the idea. Economically, Brexit has been a bit of a failure. It should come as little surprise then to find out that politically it's not going great either. The latest jug of polling suggests that 61% of Brits think Brexit has been more of a failure than a success, compared with just 13% who think it's been more of a success. Similarly, 56% of Brits think it was wrong for Britain to vote to leave the EU, while only 31% continue to say it was the right decision. But despite all this, Brexit is politically speaking the dog that won't bark. Labour don't want to talk about it, and it doesn't seem to have hurt Nigel Farage electorally, despite the fact that roughly 2/3 of Brits hold him responsible for Brexit's failures. This is for a variety of reasons, including that Labour is scared of a topic that has proved electorally toxic for them in the past and that any deepening of the existing UK EU relationship would require difficult negotiations with the EU who were understandably scarred by the chaos of Brexit. Nonetheless, if economic growth continues to prove elusive and regret continues to grow, political gravity might push Star into rethinking the current arrangement. We're taking a break from regularly posting over the festive period. But if you did want more from TLDDR, then check out our magazine Tool Long, which is also available as a digital edition, so you can start reading instantly. Digging down into the data, we see that wages have roughly doubled since 1990, while house prices have roughly quadrupled. More specifically, if we have a look at the average wage to average house price ratio over the last 75 years, it generally stood at between four and six. Meaning the average house generally cost between 4 and 6 times the average wage. In 2024, though, the average house in the UK costs 7.7 times the average salary, rising to 11 in London. This means that fewer people can afford to make it onto the housing ladder, and those who do do it later in life. While home ownership rates have declined among all working age groups, this drop is most pronounced amongst the younger age bracket. Back in 1994, just under 60% of those aged 25 to 34 owned their own homes. But by 2022, this had fallen to around 40%. For under 25s, the home ownership rate dropped from 24% in the early 2000s to just 10% in 2023. All of this has meant that the average age of house buying in the UK is now 34, an all-time high. As such, many younger people choose to rent instead. But even this is inordinately expensive, with the ONS showing that since 2016, average private rents have consistently remained above 30% of the median income, what they consider affordable. So with few affordable options available to them, younger people are increasingly opting to stay at home with their parents. Around a third of those aged between 25 and 27 lived with their parents in 2023, up from 20% in the early 2000s. This is all pretty depressing for younger people. For those that rent, it means that they're throwing more of their paycheck to a landlord. And for those that live at home, it delays them from starting a family of their own, likely contributing to the UK's fertility crisis. Considering these weren't issues for the older generations, it's easy to look at this as an example of intergenerational unfairness. While fewer younger people are buying houses, the ones that are are increasingly from wealthy families with the Bank of Mad supporting their purchases. This didn't used to be the case. But by 2020, 30% of first-time buyers relied on parental wealth, and 5% had their parents buy the property for them. Now, let's move on to the second area in which younger generations are losing out, employment. While this figure doesn't seem that high compared to the youth unemployment rate post 2008 crash, there are signs that things are starting to get worse. For instance, this year only 27% of final year students compared to 33% in 2023 had secured a job by February of their graduating year. Perhaps, as some of the media would have us believe, this is simply because Gen Zed are lazy and aren't putting enough effort into finding a job. But this doesn't seem to be the case. The first seems to be the rise of AI, which employers are using to replace entry-level jobs. Research from KCL found that firms whose workforces are highly exposed to AI capabilities reduced total employment by 4 and a.5% on average between 2021 and 2025 with the effect concentrated almost entirely in junior positions, which fell by 5.8%. Making matters worse, the same report also found that workplaces that are highly exposed to AI were 16.3 percentage points less likely to post new vacancies. Now, these problems are already bad enough, but what makes them worse is that even when younger people do get a job, their economic prospects remain lower than most. Firstly, when younger people do get jobs, they're often not paid that well. But low pay is still prevalent among 16 to 24 year olds. And secondly, there's also evidence that early career unemployment can lower your earnings later in life, something known as unemployment scarring. According to economists at the University of Leicester and the University of Nottingham, each month of unemployment between the ages of 18 and 20 leads to a permanent income loss of 1.2% over the course of a career. So, not only are younger people finding it harder to get a job, which is hurting their pay in the long term, but even when they do get a job, it's often not even that well paid. So, let's move on to the third part of this video, education. An obvious place to start here is by looking at the cost of university. This might be familiar territory for any of our longtime viewers or other Brexit nerds, but it's been a while since we've talked about it, so we think it's worth a quick recap. Inshore, a customs union is a type of trade agreement in which a group of countries agree to do two things. Firstly, remove tariffs on all trade between themselves and secondly, adopt a common external tariff. In other words, agree to charge the same tariff rates on imports from countries outside of the union. This makes trade within the customs union a lot easier. Both because you don't have to pay tariffs internally, but also because in adopting a common external tariff, you also don't need so-called rules of origin. For context, rules of origin are used to determine where an import originally came from. Even if country A has a free trade agreement with country B, but not a common external tariff, you would need these rules of origin to make sure that the correct tariff is paid when country A has a different external tariff to country B. For example, at the moment there is zero tariffs on electric vehicles between the UK and EU, which means you can easily export an electric vehicle from the UK to the EU and vice versa. However, the EU has imposed some pretty steep tariffs on electric vehicles imported from China, while the UK has imposed zero tariffs on electric vehicles imported from China. This creates a potential loophole. Chinese companies could just export their electric vehicles to the UK before sneaking them into Europe, paying zero tariffs on the way. To block off these loopholes, you have rules of origin, which basically require importers and exporters to provide paperwork proving where the product actually originated from to make sure that it's paying the correct tariff. The point we're trying to make is that rules of origin make trade more difficult, even if you've got zero tariffs. So, in some sense, a customs union is an upgrade on your bog standard free trade area. Consequently, 56% of Brits now support rejoining the EU. While this isn't going to happen anytime soon, 49% of Brits also support rejoining the customs union compared to just 19% who oppose it. You get the idea. This government is very much led by what the polls say. And the polls say that rejoining the customs union is popular. Now, that's not to say that it's definitely going to happen. Star has continued to rule it out, and it's worth noting that the UK EU reset isn't going brilliantly at the moment. It would also complicate the UK's recent trade deals, including with the US. In the second, we're going to have a look at some of the supply side issues and how governments over the last 50 years created them. And in the third, we're going to have a look at the demand side issues. Again, taking a look at how certain decisions by governments of the past caused these. So, let's start by having a look at whether there is a crisis in the first place. One of the best metrics to track the cost of housing over time is the ratio of the average house price to the average wage. Between 1960 and the end of the 20th century, the average house was roughly four to six times the average salary. In the 21st century, this has slowly increased up to a high in 2021 of nine times the average salary. The last time the average house to wage ratio was this high was £1876. While the ratio has slightly decreased since, it still stands at 7.7 with the average house costing £290,000 in 2024 and the average wage being only £37,600. In effect, this basically means that since 1997, the average wage has doubled while the average house price has increased by more than four times. Now, at this point, any remaining optimists in the audience might ask, is this really a UK specific problem? Aren't countries all over the world struggling with a housing crisis? And well, yes, there is something of a global housing crisis at the moment, but the UK seems to be struggling with it more than others. According to the latest demographia report, for instance, the ratio between the median house and the median income in the UK is higher than basically any other angophone country apart from Australia and New Zealand. Analysis by the homebuilders federation shows that the UK has the most number of people overburdened by housing costs in Europe, which they define as being anyone who pays more than 40% of their income on housing. Back in 2011, 15.8% 8% of the population were overburdened by housing and by 2020 this had risen to 19.9% 12 points ahead of the EU average of 8%. In the same year only 5.6% of people in France were overburdened by housing and only 9% of Germans were overburdened. It's not as though British citizens get better houses for their money either. English homes tend to have an average floor space per person of 38 m squared which is less than other similar countries like the US where it's 66 m squared, Germany where it's 46 m squared and France where it's 43 m squared. In fact, people living in England have on average even less floor space per person than someone living in New York. Similarly, the UK has the highest share of pre-war housing stock of any European country, meaning that houses are usually harder to insulate and have more problems with damp. So, why is this the case? Why does the UK perform so badly when it comes to housing? Well, a lot of this can be explained by the fact that the UK just hasn't built enough homes. The UK has only 446 homes per 1,000 people, significantly below the European average of 542 homes per thousand people. Clearly then, if the UK has the oldest housing stock in Europe and the highest proportion of pre-war homes, something went wrong between the Second World War and now that stopped us building. With this in mind, let's move on and have a look at some of the supply side issues that have restricted house building. To do this, we need to go back to the 1930s when house builders were able to access cheap material and cheap labor. Back then, the UK had minimal building restrictions, essentially just those that enforced minimum standards on space and sanitation. As a result of all this, house building exploded, doubling between 1932 and 1934 from 142,000 homes a year to 286,000 a year. Such a rapid increase, though caused some problems. Those who lived on the outskirts of cities grew frustrated with urban sprawl and formed the Council for the Protection of Rural England or CPRE, a group designed to try and push the government to prevent the growth of cities. As a result of the group's influence, the head of the CPRE, Patrick Abocrombi, was commissioned to write a report on regional inequality by then Prime Minister Neville Chamberlain in which he argued for the decentralization of industry and labor out of cities and towards state managed new towns. The recommendations from the report were accepted by the Labor government in 1947 in the towns and country planning act. This was the beginning of the modern planning system as we know it. There were two main changes made in the act, each of which hurt house building, albeit in different ways. The first was setting the stage for the creation of so-called green belts. These are donut-shaped regions that surround cities in which house building was heavily restricted, which was an attempt to stop urban sprawl. In many ways, these green belts have been wildly successful. They continue to prevent house building in the areas surrounding cities. Only 0.3% of the green belt in 2022 was taken up by residential buildings. And by 2023, they covered over 16,000 km squared, roughly 12.6% of the land in England, and more than double the 7,000 km squared or so they covered in 1979. The second change the 1947 system made was to move the UK from a zonal planning system to a discretionary planning system. Under the zonal system, as long as new developments meet land use plans, they're automatically granted permission. Under the discretionary system, local authorities can deny planning permission even if they meet land use plans. At the same time, the government essentially nationalized house building. Only private house building that was in the public interest would be granted. This was mainly because rationing was still enforced and the government wanted to make sure materials were being used wisely. Instead, the government expanded public house building, much of which was focused on the development of new towns located outside cities and designed to prevent demand for the development in cities. The issue was that when public house building in new towns slowed, the discretionary system was relied upon to provide new homes via the private sector. The discretionary planning system provides very little incentives for people to actually accept new houses in their area. People simply didn't like house building in their areas. It would often create additional strain on local services and infrastructure and often negatively affect their own house price. They had little reason to approve house building and plenty of reason and opportunity to block it. Added on top of this was the fact that from 1971 onwards, the majority of people in the UK owned their own homes, so actually benefited from a lack of building, which meant less housing supply and thus higher house prices. This meant that there wasn't any substantial pressure to actually change the system and private sector house building reliably disappointed. To make matters worse, while the private sector was struggling to build, the public housing stock was actively shrinking largely as a consequence of the infamous right to buy scheme under which council house tenants are given the right to purchase their homes. Prior to Margaret Thatcher's election in 1979, local authorities had the ability to sell to their tenants, but such sales were generally limited. In 1980, Thatcher massively scaled up the right to buy offering, providing discounts starting at 33% and rising to 50% for tenants who'd lived in their council house for 20 years or more. Since these discounts were introduced, more than 2 million council homes have been sold. The issue here though was that councils only got a fraction of what they would have got had the property been sold at market value. And on top of this, they were prevented from using the money to build additional stock. The Local Government and Housing Act of 1989 required councils to direct as much as 75% of the proceeds to reducing council debt. As a result, only 2% of the houses that were sold via right to buy were ever replaced, and council house completions essentially collapsed under Thatcher and then New Labor. In fact, a Greater London Authority report in 2019 found that 42% of homes purchased under right to buy ended up being privately rented. What this has meant is that the UK has gone from subsidizing low-income housing through council houses to subsidizing lowincome tenants to rent privately. more and more of those on low incomes are now supported by local housing allowance or LHA, which is a government benefit linked to the bottom 30th percentile of rental properties. The issue is that of the last 13 years, LHA has been frozen in eight of them, meaning that those on the lowest incomes in the UK have to make up the difference themselves. Taken together, the changes made in 1947 mean that the UK just hasn't built enough houses. A 2017 government white paper puts it simply. The UK needs 225,000 to 275,000 new houses annually, but since the 1970s has only averaged 160,000. Some research even suggests that had the UK built houses at the rate of average European countries from 1955 to 2015, the UK would have an additional 4.3 million homes. However, while the root cause of the housing crisis might well be a lack of supply, government policy also made things worse by increasing demand without doing anything about supply. The introduction of btolet mortgages in the '90s alongside financial deregulation brought in by new labor encouraged aspiring landlords to buy up council houses and other properties, pushing up prices and squeezing out wouldbe homeowners. Help to buy, which basically involved the government subsidizing first-time buyers, was originally intended as a small 250 million pound scheme when it was first announced by the tries in 2010, but had ballooned to about30 billion by the time it was wound down in 2022. Recent studies have found that by essentially pouring public money into the housing market, help to buy just pushed up prices in supply constrained areas like greater London without doing anything to stimulate construction. All in all, while the main cause of the UK housing crisis seems to be the changes to housing legislation made in the 40s and 50s, which made it harder to build in the UK, more recent government policy hasn't really helped either. To their credit, the current government does seem to have at least recognized that the only sustainable solution is to build more houses. But they're a long way off their stated target of building 1 and a half million new homes over this parliament. If you enjoyed this video and you'd like another on the government's new plans, let us know in the comments below. Now, that's quite a lot of information we've thrown your way. For context, fiscal rules are self-imposed rules that limit how much a government can spend or borrow. And Reeves and Star set themselves some pretty stringent ones in order to signal to both markets and skeptical voters that they could be trusted with the economy. Technically, they set themselves three fiscal rules, but the most restrictive one and the one that forced Reeves to raise taxes at the budget is the so-called stability rule. This rule requires that by the fiscal year ending 2030, day-to-day spending, which essentially means all government spending apart from capital investments is met by tax revenues. Anyway, to see whether the government is on track to meet this target, every six months, the Office for Budget Responsibility, the UK's Independent Fiscal Watchdog, usually just known as the OBR, lays out a forecast. This is a bit of a simplification, but for the purposes of this particular fiscal rule, the OBR essentially forecasts two things. How much the government is going to take in in taxes in 2030 and how much the government is going to spend on day-to-day expenditures in 2030. And broadly speaking, for the fiscal rule to be met, the first number has to be bigger than the second. The difference between these two numbers is euphemistically known as the headroom. The problem for Reeves has always been that at her very first budget last year, she only carved out a tiny 10 billion pound headroom. This was always at risk because it meant that even a slight downgrade in the OBR's forecast could wipe out the headroom and force Reeves to either suddenly raise taxes or cut spending. This is why since the OBR's creation in 2010, previous governments have always tried to carve out larger headrooms. And well, this is exactly what happened. 6 months later, at the next forecast, the OBR announced that because the UK economy wasn't doing as well as they originally expected, the headroom had been wiped out. This forced Reeves into a sort of emergency budget. But even then, she again only eaked out about 10 billion or so in headroom. Again, this wasn't enough. And like they did 6 months earlier, ahead of this budget, the OBR once again downgraded their forecasts, wiping out most of the headroom. Broadly speaking, this left Reeves with two options, one simple and one complicated. The simple option was just to raise a big pre-existing tax like income tax. This would raise a lot of money and probably give Reeves enough headroom to avoid being forced into another emergency budget in the near future. This would however require Labor to break their manifesto pledge to not raise taxes on quote working people which is widely understood to preclude increases on income tax, national insurance or VAT. This means something like 10 billion pounds more in income tax for the government because when inflation and wages rise, more people move into higher bands. Although, we should note that this is effectively regressive because it hits poorer people moving into higher bands and doesn't affect anyone who's already paying the top rate. On top of that, there was also a tax increase on salary sacrifice pension contributions expected to raise about4 billion. New taxes on gambling companies which are expected to raise about1 billion. A twopoint increase to the basic and higher rates of tax on dividends coming in April 2026, which isn't a bad idea given that dividends are currently taxed at a lower rate than income. A council tax sir charge on houses valued over2 million pounds coming in in 2028 expected to raise about500 million pounds. A so-called road tax for electric vehicles charged at 3p per mile from 2028 onwards and then rising with inflation. And finally, there are a number of smaller taxes, including an expanded sugar tax nicknamed the milkshake tax, a tourism tax charged at 2 a night for stays in hotels and other holiday rentals, and a taxi tax introducing VATs for private taxi firms. All in all, these new taxes will raise something like 26 billion by 2030. This would have taken the overall headroom to just over30 billion. But Reeds also announced a number of new spending commitments, including most notably scrapping the two child benefit cap and increasing benefits in line with inflation, which took the overall headroom down to about 22 billion. This is obviously bigger than the 10 billion that re had before. That's just not that big by historical standards, and the OBR have warned that the headroom is still quote small given the uncertainties around its forecast as a result of all of these complex tax changes. It's also very possible that some of these taxes don't actually happen. After all, you can easily imagine an unpopular Labor government shying away from potentially unpopular taxes, like say the new road tax for electric vehicles when they're due in a couple of years time. As a final thing, while going for the complicated option might be politically understandable, it is economically suboptimal to say the least. Politically, the complicated option allowed Labor to stick to their manifesto and by delaying a lot of these new taxes until 2027 and 2028 helped an embattled government avoid some of the immediate political pain. But economically, the complicated option has added yet more complexity to the UK's already super complicated tax system when a simple option like raising income tax, which is what Reeves implied she was going to do a couple of weeks ago, would have been cleaner, more progressive, raised more money, and push down inflation. Some of the other budget measures, like the cut to energy levies on energy bills or the freezing of fuel duty, are clearly intended to bring down inflation. But they achieve this by reducing rather than raising tax receipts and add yet more complexity to the sax system. Moreover, it's not even clear that this will work politically. As older viewers might remember, George Osborne's attempt at a similar smorgas budget back in 2012 backfired spectacularly, sparking a stream of unflattering headlines about specific taxes like the infamous pasti tax and ultimately being nicknamed the omni shambles budget. All in all, this has put Starmmer's government in a dangerous position. Houses are appallingly expensive, growth is sluggish, inflation is still high, and the public finances clearly aren't in good nick. However, it's important to recognize that things could definitely be worse. For starters, the UK was actually the fastest growing economy in the G7 for the first half of this year. And while growth has slowed somewhat since both the IMF and the OECD forecast that the UK will be the second fastest growing economy in the G7 in 2025, behind only the US and roughly on a par once you strip out AI related spending on data centers, which are currently adding something like 0.5% to America's GDP. Furthermore, while our public finances aren't in great nick, it's important not to overdo it. Borrowing costs may be high relative to what they were in the 2010s, but compared to what they used to be, they're actually really not that high at all. Compared to 30% in the US, 47% in France and Italy, and 48% in Germany. A smidge above the US, but well below Italy, France, and Germany. The point we're trying to make here is that the UK's public finances are squeezed. Sure, but unlike, say, France, the UK still has a lot more room to tax. Furthermore, there are at least three reasons to be optimistic about the future. First, the UK relies far less on manufacturing than most other countries and more on services like finance, law, and consulting. It's the second largest service exporter in the world after only the US with services exports roughly doubling between 2016 and 2025, overtaking goods exports. This means the UK is far better placed to cope with the coming era of protectionism than other countries. Take Germany for instance, which relies far more on goods than services. Tariffs and other protectionist policies implemented by countries like the US and China have made it far harder for German manufacturers to export to other markets and compete with their subsidized competitors in the US and China. The UK is less affected because well these protectionist barriers only affect goods not services. This is why UK US trade has held up relatively well. The UK exported 59 billion pounds of goods to the US in 2024 but exported more than twice as much 137 billion pounds in services which weren't affected by Trump's tariffs. Second, the UK is actually a remarkably entrepreneurial and innovative economy. Take the global innovation index for instance, which uses 80 metrics to rank countries on their levels of innovation. These are split into innovation inputs such as R&D, education expenditure, and number of STEM graduates and innovation outputs such as patents, high-tech exports, and finance available to startups. Overall, the UK ranks as sixth globally in the index, putting it ahead of China, France, and Germany. On inputs specifically, the UK ranks 10th, and on outputs, it ranks third, which suggests that the UK is getting quite a lot of value from its innovation investment. The UK comes behind only the US, China, and India when it comes to the number of unicorn companies it has. Companies valued at $1 billion or more and punches above its weight in a number of high-tech industries including AI, quantum technology, and pharma. Quite a lot of this is down to the UK's innovation clusters like London, Cambridge, Oxford, and Manchester, areas where academia, business, and investment all work together to produce new technologies and ultimately economic growth. The government has recognized the value of these clusters and recently announced a plan to try and better connect Oxford and Cambridge through the creation of more homes and better transport links with a hope that it becomes Europe's Silicon Valley. Third, the UK has a lot of what we might call catch-up potential. One of the root causes of the UK's economic woes, for instance, is its weak investment levels. Higher investment typically means businesses find innovative ways to improve productivity, which in turn helped to improve economic output. In the 1980s, investment accounted for about 23% of UK GDP. From the 2000s onwards, this had fallen to around 17% and has not recovered. This hasn't been the case in France and Germany, where investment has remained between 20 and 25%. The original decline in the '90s was probably something to do with the UK's transition away from manufacturing towards a service-based economy, but the prolonged stagnation in the 2000s was at least partly caused by austerity, and the slump in the 2010s and 20s was at least partly caused by Brexit and the ensuing uncertainty. This has been terrible for the UK's productivity. While the UK enjoyed relatively similar productivity to France and Germany in the early 2000s, the UK has since fallen behind with the gap between the UK and France and Germany tripling between 2007 and 2019. But this all comes with a substantial silver lining. Austerity and Brexit are finally over, which means investment levels should start rising again. In fact, this year we've already seen a 3.7% boost in private sector investment and public sector investment is set to reach almost 140 billion pounds by 2027, almost twice what it was back in 2008. If the UK is able to get back on track and increase its productivity to the levels of France and Germany, this will provide an extra 100 billion pounds per year for the Treasury or around £8,000 per household. The silver lining we're trying to articulate here is that a massive increase in living standards wouldn't require a completely unrealistic transformation of the economy. Rather, it just requires us to become a more normal country in terms of investment and productivity. Anyway, you get the point. The UK economy isn't doing as bad as it's sometimes made out, and there is cause for optimism going forward. So, let's move on to the second area where the UK might be doing better than you think. Education. Again, this is just something the UK does very well. The UK has four of the top 10 universities in the world according to the QS World University Rankings for this year. This is particularly beneficial for two reasons. First, it's good for the economy. Foreign students pay more to study here in the first place, and at least some of them go on to start businesses in the UK and help grow the economy. But second, it also provides the UK with a significant amount of soft power. Having some of the world's most prestigious universities means that we attract and educate people who go on to be incredibly influential. For instance, the UK has educated 50 world leaders who are in post in 2022. More national leaders than any other country in the world. But it's not just universities. British schools are also pretty great. Now, this wasn't always the case. By 2022, however, the latest year for which PISA data is available, British students had risen to 12th in maths, 14th in science, and 13th in reading in the PISA rankings. the best results for any large European country apart from Poland. A year later, 88% of schools were ranked as either good or outstanding. Behind this improvement was the Conservative Schools Plan, which was implemented by Schools Minister Nick Gibb. In many ways, this is a demonstration of what government can do when it comes up with a plan and actually sticks to it. Now, while this isn't something people tend to be that optimistic about here, there's at least one reason they should be, and that's the UK's first pass the post electoral system. More generally, the UK has somehow preserved its international standing. Similar trends are illustrated among under 35s by the Economists UK poll tracker which has found that while Labour won nearly half the under 35's vote at the 2024 election, it now holds only a quarter. Meanwhile, the Greens have surged, overtaking Labor among 18 to 34 year olds and now holding around 30% of the under 35 vote. So why are young people ditching Labor for the Greens? Well, as we see it, the exodus of young voters has been driven by Labour's handling of at least four issues that young people care about. The Israel Palestine conflict, immigration, the economy, and the housing crisis. The second reason young voters are turning on Labor is the party's stance on immigration. Research published in 2023 by the Migration Observatory at the University of Oxford found that young people are more likely to say immigration is good or very good than bad or very bad.
