Similarly, when asked how the number of immigrants to the UK should change, 18 to 24 year olds were far less likely than older age groups to say it should be reduced a lot, with half of 18 to 24 year olds and 41% of 25 to 34 year olds saying it should either be reduced a little or remain the same. Labour's confused messaging on immigration with Star attracting criticism earlier this year for suggesting that the UK risked becoming an island of strangers before later backtracking and then using his September conference speech to condemn racist violence and hatred again suggests that Labour lacks a coherent stance on immigration which is pushing young voters who are generally more supportive of immigration towards the Greens with their staunchly pro-immigration platform. The third and fourth reasons that young voters are switching from Labor to the Greens is Labour's apparent lack of progress on the economy and housing. While these are two separate issues, we'll cover them here as one point, as they're both part of the same cost of living crisis affecting young people. For context, as this Yug of Tracker shows, the economy is now by far the most important issue for 18 to 24 year olds, while for British adults overall, it's joint top with immigration and asylum. Housing, meanwhile, has risen steadily in importance for 18 to 24 year olds in recent years with around 20% citing it as a top issue in 2021 compared to 27% now. This differs from UK adults more generally, among whom the trend line for housing as a top issue has remained relatively flat. This is probably because young people in the UK tend to spend a far higher proportion of their income on rent and are more likely than older age groups to live in privately rented accommodation, which is often more expensive than other options. According to the UK government's 2021-22 English housing survey, among private renters, those aged between 16 to 24 spend as much as 44% of their income on rent, the highest of any age group. At the 2024 election, among voters who rent privately, Labour received three times the vote share, 42% of any other party, suggesting the party was elected partly on its pledge to deliver more affordable homes. However, since the election, Labour's house project appears to have stalled, with a party currently on course to miss its 1.5 million target. Moreover, while Labour's renters rights bill has restricted landlords power to raise rents, the party has ruled out further actions like introducing rent controls, a government set cap on rent levels or increases, despite Yugov polling for Commonwealth in 2024 finding that 75% of UK adults back the policy and only a tiny fraction more of landlords oppose rather than support rent controls. ## 30. Why Starmer’s Removal Looks Increasingly Inevitable

Nonetheless, there's long been a sense that for all his shortcomings, Starmmer isn't going anywhere. After all, he's still got a stalking majority in the Commons and is just the fourth Labour leader in British political history to actually win an election. Unfortunately for Star, that's apparently changed and Labour MPs have been telling Westminster Journals that this situation has become terminal. Rather, we're saying that there are now a number of structural factors that are constantly dragging Star down, making it near impossible for him to recover. There are at least three structural factors. The first is what we might call his budget dilemma. The second is that he's now so unpopular that he can't recover. And the third is that he lacks a long-term project to convince Labour MPs and voters to stick with him. Let's start with a budget dilemma. In short, Starmmer boxed himself in at the last general election by doing two things. First, promising not to raise any major taxes. and second setting himself some relatively strict fiscal rules limiting how much he can borrow. Importantly, these borrowing limits are based on forecasts by the Office for Budget Responsibility or OBR which happen every 6 months. This might have worked if the UK economy had suddenly boomed. But while the UK is fairing better than some of its peers, the global economy as a whole has slowed, largely thanks to Trump's unorthodox trade policies, taking the UK with it. In practice, this has meant that every six months when the OBR releases a new forecast showing that things haven't got better, to adhere to its own fiscal rules, the government currently has to either tinker with some minor taxes to raise revenue or cut spending. This is clearly unsustainable. Businesses don't like the government constantly fiddling with taxes, and neither voters nor Labor MPs like spending cuts. This has left Starmmer with a budget dilemma. He can either continue to stumble on and desperately hope that the UK economy suddenly booms, which feels unlikely, or at the next budget, he can break one of the two promises he made during the campaign. In other words, either raise one of the major taxes, like income tax, or do away with his fiscal rules. Of these two options, raising taxes looks more feasible, largely because the bond markets don't really want to lend the UK much more money anyway, at least not without very high interest rates. At the moment, it seems that Starmer is planning to bite the bullet and raise taxes. But while this might be the lesser of two evils, it's still pretty terrible for Star. Breaking manifesto commitments isn't always the end of the world, but it's a bad look when A, it's a commitment that everyone knows about and that you reiterated hundreds of times, and B, you don't have a good excuse for breaking it. Star and Reeves are currently trying to blame Brexit, but this doesn't really fly because everybody already knew about the negative economic consequences of Brexit back when Starmmer made those promises. This will also make Starmmer's removal even more tempting for Labour MPs because Star can take the fall for the tax rises and a new leader can reap the political rewards of that newfound fiscal space. The second structural issue facing Starmmer is his unpopularity. As a politician, being a bit unpopular isn't the end of the world. You can just do popular things to turn the tide. Star's allies in parliament, for instance, like to point to Margaret Thatcher's first term. Like Star, her first year was a bit of a disaster and her approval rating slumped. But then she started doing popular things. Her approval ratings improved and she went on to win two more elections. The problem with this analogy though is thatcher was only quite unpopular. Even at her lowest moments in her first and second terms, her approval ratings never really dropped below 30%. And her net approval rating rarely fell below minus 35. Starmer is way less popular. Most pollsters currently put his approval rating below 20%. And his net approval rating somewhere between -40 and -50. And these numbers will probably fall further after the tax rises at the budget. A recent Ipsos poll put his approval rating at just 13%. With a net rating of minus 66, the lowest satisfaction rating recorded by the pollster for any prime minister going back to 1977. When you're this unpopular, even when you do popular things, instead of those things making you more popular, then newfound association with you makes the things themselves less popular. We're already seeing this with Star. Digital ID, for instance, was actually a pretty popular policy before Star endorsed it, at which point the public turned on it. A poll in June found that 53% of voters were in favor of digital ID cards for all Britons, with just 19% against. Polling after Star's endorsement, however, found that the percentage in favor had fallen to 31% with 45% now against. So, you get the idea. There comes a point where you're so unpopular that there's no way out. Anyway, the third structural problem for Star is that he doesn't have a long-term vision or project to convince not just voters, but also Labour MPs that all this pain is worth it. Watch this over on the TLDDR podcast channel. It's linked below. Now, it's worth starting by explaining what the ECR actually is. In the aftermath of the Second World War, European nations met to try and draft a treaty that ensured that the horrors of World War II could never be repeated. As a result of these conversations, in 1949, the ECR was formally drafted. And in 1951, the United Kingdom became the first country to officially ratify the convention. Included in the ECR is everything from the right to life article 2, the freedom of expression, article 10, and prohibition of discrimination, article 14. In order to ensure that these rights are upheld in the signaturary countries, in 1959, the European Court of Human Rights was established. Citizens who believe that their rights have been denied can have their case heard at the court in Strasburg. The next big development came in 1998. This is when the UK's Human Rights Act was introduced under the new Labor government. This law essentially incorporated the ECR into domestic law. While this doesn't actually change any of the rights afforded to citizens, it does mean that citizens are able to bring cases to UK courts if they feel that their rights have been violated. If they're unsuccessful, the cases can still though be heard at Strasburg. This all sounds pretty good though. So why do Reform and the Conservatives want to leave it? For both Reform UK and the Conservatives, their main issue with the ECR is how it impacts the government's ability to control immigration. While the ECR doesn't itself prevent states from being able to restrict immigration, those on the right claim that it's been used to block deportations and that the only way to stop this is by either disapplying elements of it or leaving it entirely. There are two articles in particular that they claim are used for this purpose. Article three, the prohibition of torture or inhumane treatment, and article 8, the protection of private and family life. Article 3 means that governments can't deport a migrant to a country where there's a high risk that they'd face persecution, ill treatment, or other serious human rights violations, known in international law as non-refilement. It's important to add here that non-refilement is a core principle of international law, also enshrined in the UN refugee convention, to which the UK is a signary. So leaving the ECR wouldn't obviously end the UK's commitment to it. Anyway, article 8 means that the government should attempt to protect the right to a private and family life. The important thing to note here though is that this is a qualified right, not an absolute right. What this means is that the convention allows states to violate this right if they have a legitimate reason. And controlling immigration and deporting those that are seen as a threat to public safety are understood as legitimate reasons. According to a report from University of Oxford's Bonavo Institute of Human Rights, since 1980, the European Court of Human Rights has only found against the UK in 13 removal cases. And despite much of the coverage this year being about Article 8 in particular, only four of these were connected to the right to a family life. When it comes to the Human Rights Act itself, things are a little more murky. The Home Office doesn't regularly publish deportation data. The latest data released was in 2021. This though showed that only 922 foreign national offenders successfully challenged their deportation on human rights grounds in the first tier tribunal between April 2016 and June 2021. Only 3.5% of the total deportations. And when we consider that cases can be reviewed by an upper tribunal that's able to overturn these successful challenges, the number is likely even lower. Unfortunately, there isn't any formal data on this. Though the point we're making is that while leaving the ECR might help with deportations, the effects will likely be small. Anyway, with this in mind, how could the UK actually leave? Now, it is possible for the UK to denounce or leave the ECR. In order to do so, 5 years needs to have passed since it joined, and 6 months notice needs to be given to the Secretary General of the Council of Europe. If the UK were to do this, it would be the third country ever to leave the ECR. The first being Greece who left when they were under the rule of a military juna and who later rejoined and then Russia who was expelled following its full-scale invasion of Ukraine. And in addition to this, British citizens would lose the external scrutiny of their human rights provided to them by a super national court. This would happen even if the government introduced a British bill of rights as the conservatives previously suggested. Clearly leaving would certainly be a reputational risk and could come with a weakening of the protection of rights for UK citizens. In addition to this though, there are two other significant obstacles to leaving. The potential for it to undermine both the Good Friday Agreement and the Trade and Cooperation Agreement. The issue is that the Good Friday Agreement contains a clause in it that states that the ECR should be incorporated into Northern Ireland law along with further references throughout the document to safeguards provided by the ECR. Some right-wing think tanks have suggested that the Good Friday Agreement could simply be reinterpreted. But other legal experts have hit back at this, suggesting that the ECR is fundamental to the Good Friday Agreement. This is something that even Nigel Farage appears to accept with him pointing out that a government led by him would renegotiate the terms of the Good Friday Agreement in order to get the ECR out of it. In addition to this though, leaving the ECR would also break some elements of the UK EU Trade and Cooperation Agreement or the TCA. Article 763 of the treaty states that the parties reaffirm their respect for the universal declaration of human rights and the international human rights treaties to which they're parties. Breaking this would allow the EU to terminate or suspend the operation of the agreement. Now, it's unlikely that the EU would tear up the entire agreement, but it very much could lead to the suspension of areas of judicial cooperation. For example, the suspension of extradition. So all in all, leaving the ECHR wouldn't help to increase deportations by as much as you might think, and it would come with some significant obstacles. It was first introduced in the First World War in order to help authorities manage conscription and was then reintroduced in 1939 to help with national service, national security, and food rationing. National ID was however abolished in 1952 after it was criticized for being used for more general purposes. for example, with law enforcement, and opponents argued that this wasn't compatible with civil liberties. The policy re-entered the national conversation in the early 2000s when Tony Blair proposed a new voluntary entitlement card. Now, it's important to note just how extensive this proposal was. There's talk of a40 billion pound fiscal black hole and she's still constrained by Labour's manifesto promise last year not to raise taxes on working people which has been interpreted to mean raising income tax, national insurance and VAT which together account for 40% of total tax receipts. As a result of all this, journalists and economists seem to think that Reeves will need to get creative in the budget to try and raise tax revenue without causing too much financial pain to those that can't afford it. So, in this video, we're going to look into one of these ideas, starting by explaining some of the issues with council tax as it stands, two separate proposed property taxes, and whether either of these could actually work. [Music] Dive deeper into politics with our magazine, too long. Yes, we make a real physical magazine. In the latest issue, we discuss if Starmmet just has no vision for Britain, if Raj can become prime minister, and ask if we're observing the end of the American order. Find out more by clicking the link in the description. So, let's start by explaining council tax. Now, essentially, each property is assigned a council tax band from A to H. The band is determined by the value of the property in 1991. Any house with a value of up to £40,000 in 1991 is in band A. Anything from £40,01 to £52,000 is in band B. Anything from £52,01 to £68,000 is in band C, etc. All the way up to £320,000 plus, which is band H. Any properties built since 1991 are given an antecedent valuation date or AVD, which is essentially the value the property could have sold for in 1991. Now, there were some pretty obvious issues with this. First, a lot's changed since 1991. Some areas have increased in value and some decreased. In fact, the IFS has estimated that households in the North and Midlands are in too high a band and pay too much, while households in London are in too low band and pay too little. This can be exacerbated by the fact that each council sets their own rates, meaning that a house in the same band will pay vastly different amounts in different areas. Second, the bands mean that there's an upper limit to the tax irrespective of the value of your property. As we mentioned a second ago, to be in band H, a property needs to have a 1991 value of £320,000 or roughly £880,000 today. However, if the house is valued at £1 million, £2 million, or even £10 million today, as many houses are, they still fall into band H and will pay the same in council tax, assuming they're in the same local authority. In practice, this means council tax is appallingly regressive with lower-income households in poorer areas generally paying a higher effective tax rate than higher income households in richer areas. An LSE analysis, for instance, found that poorer households pay 5% of their gross household income on average towards council tax, whereas the richest only pay 1%. This isn't the case with income tax or national insurance where generally speaking the richer you get the higher the proportion of your income you pay towards the tax. Clearly council tax is a rubbish tax which is part of the reason that the media and economists have suggested that some kind of shakeup should be included in Rachel Reeves budget. Not only would this make the UK's tax system less regressive, replacing council tax with a well-designed property tax could also ease the UK's housing crisis without discouraging work as many other taxes like income tax do. Now, a report from UK onwards, a centeright think tank outlined one potential way this property tax could work. Essentially, their suggestion was for a horizontal property tax that would replace not only council tax but also stamp duty, a tax paid when purchasing a property. Stamp duty is also a rubbish tax because it discourages people from moving house, which in turn exacerbates the UK's housing crisis. Anyway, the new tax would be designed to balance payments to local and national government while also trying to improve fairness to taxpayers. Essentially, while local governments could set the rate, they suggest that homeowners would pay an average of 0.4 44% of the value of their property up to half a million to local government each year with a minimum payment of £800. The value of any property between half a million and£1 million would be charged at 0.54% and a paid to the national government and anything in excess of a million would be charged at 0.81% and paid to the national government. UK onwards suggests that this proposal is preferable for a number of reasons. Firstly, it's significantly fairer than the current system as it's a progressive rather than a regressive tax. Second, the council tax part of the change is revenue neutral, meaning that the government would still collect the same amount of tax revenue as they do currently. And thirdly, depending on how it's implemented, it could result in fewer property valuations by local government as only properties up to half a million pounds would need valuing as this is the cap for the local government part of the tax. However, this would be a significant change. The government, despite promising to at various points, have still not even conducted a revaluation of property prices since 1991, presumably at least in part because of the fear of backlash from those that would have to pay more as a result. Therefore, perhaps it would be more realistic to look at a property tax that would merely adjust rather than completely replace the current council tax system. This is where a recent proposal from John Mulebower, a professor at Oxford University, comes in. Now, he proposed that the current council tax system remains as it is for everybody up to band G and H, the top two bands. For those in these bands, Mulebower suggests replacing the current council tax with a new annual property wealth tax of 0.5%. In total, this would affect 1.1 million properties in England and Wales. In order to mitigate the effect of taxing those that are assetri but cash poor, he suggested that payment could be deferred until the property sold. But if this is opted for, this would increase the tax to 0.6%. Now, crucially, only revenue in excess of what council tax would have raised would acrue to the central government, ensuring that no local authority loses out. The benefit of this change is that it would bring alongside a 1% tax on all agricultural and unoccupied land valued at £40,000 per hectare around £9 billion extra into the excheer each year. Additionally, as it isn't a complete overhaul of the system akin to the UK onwards proposal, it might actually be something the government could consider with the backlash being far more limited. So, while on its own this wouldn't be enough to close the fiscal black hole, it would certainly be a start and something that Reeves could consider including in an autumn budget.
